Title: Structural and functional delineation of aerobactin biosynthesis in hypervirulent Klebsiella pneumoniae.
Journal: The Journal of biological chemistry
PubDate: 2018 May 18
Authors: Bailey DC, Alexander E, Rice MR, Drake EJ, Mydy LS, Aldrich CC, Gulick AM

             </dc:title><prism:publicationName>Journal of Biological Chemistry</prism:publicationName><prism:aggregationType>Journal</prism:aggregationType><pubType>fla</pubType><prism:issn>00219258</prism:issn><prism:volume>293</prism:volume><prism:issueIdentifier>20</prism:issueIdentifier><prism:startingPage>7841</prism:startingPage><prism:endingPage>7852</prism:endingPage><prism:pageRange>7841-7852</prism:pageRange><prism:number>20</prism:number><dc:format>text/xml</dc:format><prism:coverDate>2018-05-31</prism:coverDate><prism:coverDisplayDate>May 2018</prism:coverDisplayDate><prism:copyright>© 2018 Bailey et al.</prism:copyright><prism:publisher>Bailey et al.</prism:publisher><dc:creator>Bailey, Daniel C.</dc:creator><dc:creator>Alexander, Evan</dc:creator><dc:creator>Rice, Matthew R.</dc:creator><dc:creator>Drake, Eric J.</dc:creator><dc:creator>Mydy, Lisa S.</dc:creator><dc:creator>Aldrich, Courtney C.</dc:creator><dc:creator>Gulick, Andrew M.</dc:creator><dc:description>
                  Aerobactin, a citryl-hydroxamate siderophore, is produced by a number of pathogenic Gram-negative bacteria to aid in iron assimilation. Interest in this well-known siderophore was reignited by recent investigations suggesting that it plays a key role in mediating the enhanced virulence of a hypervirulent pathotype of Klebsiella pneumoniae (hvKP). In contrast to classical opportunistic strains of K. pneumoniae, hvKP causes serious life-threatening infections in previously healthy individuals in the community. Multiple contemporary reports have confirmed fears that the convergence of multidrug-resistant and hvKP pathotypes has led to the evolution of a highly transmissible, drug-resistant, and virulent “super bug.” Despite hvKP harboring four distinct siderophore operons, knocking out production of only aerobactin led to a significant attenuation of virulence. Herein, we continue our structural and functional studies on the biosynthesis of this crucial virulence factor. In vivo heterologous production and in vitro reconstitution of aerobactin biosynthesis from hvKP was carried out, demonstrating the specificity, stereoselectivity, and kinetic throughput of the complete pathway. Additionally, we present a steady-state kinetic analysis and the X-ray crystal structure of the second aerobactin synthetase IucC, as well as describe a surface entropy reduction strategy that was employed for structure determination. Finally, we show solution X-ray scattering data that support a unique dimeric quaternary structure for IucC. These new insights into aerobactin assembly will help inform potential antivirulence strategies and advance our understanding of siderophore biosynthesis.

                     <ce:bold>The X-ray crystal structure of IucC.</ce:bold> 
                     <ce:italic>A,</ce:italic> the monomeric tertiary structure of IucC surface mutant M5 colored by domain. Gaps in the model corresponding to disordered loops (DL1–3) are connected by <ce:italic>black dashed lines</ce:italic> for clarity. Protein chain termini are highlighted by <ce:italic>spheres</ce:italic>, with the N and C termini colored <ce:italic>blue</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>red</ce:italic>, respectively. The SER patch is emphasized in <ce:italic>yellow. B,</ce:italic> close-up view of the modeled active site. The disordered loops (DL1–3, <ce:italic>magenta</ce:italic>) were modeled by overlaying corresponding regions of a homology model (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref20" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S4<ce:italic>A</ce:italic>
                     </ce:cross-ref>). ATP (<ce:italic>green carbons</ce:italic>) and Mg<ce:sup loc="post">2+</ce:sup> (<ce:italic>light green sphere</ce:italic>) from the crystal structure of IucA (PDB code <ce:inter-ref id="interrefs10" xlink:href="pdb:5JM8" xlink:type="simple">5JM8</ce:inter-ref>) are also modeled into the active site with IucC the side chains of several predicted binding residues labeled and depicted as sticks. <ce:italic>C,</ce:italic> the crystallographic asymmetric unit containing eight copies of IucC illustrated in two orthogonal views. Potential tetrameric (<ce:italic>left</ce:italic>) and dimeric (<ce:italic>right</ce:italic>) assemblies are highlighted using the same color scheme as <ce:italic>panel A</ce:italic>.</ce:simple-para>

            <ce:miscellaneous id="mi0005">Edited by Joseph M. Jez</ce:miscellaneous>
            <ce:abstract id="ceab10" view="all" class="author"><ce:abstract-sec id="ceabs10" view="all">
                  <ce:simple-para id="spara120" view="all">Aerobactin, a citryl-hydroxamate siderophore, is produced by a number of pathogenic Gram-negative bacteria to aid in iron assimilation. Interest in this well-known siderophore was reignited by recent investigations suggesting that it plays a key role in mediating the enhanced virulence of a hypervirulent pathotype of <ce:italic>Klebsiella pneumoniae</ce:italic> (hvKP). In contrast to classical opportunistic strains of <ce:italic>K. pneumoniae</ce:italic>, hvKP causes serious life-threatening infections in previously healthy individuals in the community. Multiple contemporary reports have confirmed fears that the convergence of multidrug-resistant and hvKP pathotypes has led to the evolution of a highly transmissible, drug-resistant, and virulent “super bug.” Despite hvKP harboring four distinct siderophore operons, knocking out production of <ce:italic>only</ce:italic> aerobactin led to a significant attenuation of virulence. Herein, we continue our structural and functional studies on the biosynthesis of this crucial virulence factor. <ce:italic>In vivo</ce:italic> heterologous production and <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic> reconstitution of aerobactin biosynthesis from hvKP was carried out, demonstrating the specificity, stereoselectivity, and kinetic throughput of the complete pathway. Additionally, we present a steady-state kinetic analysis and the X-ray crystal structure of the second aerobactin synthetase IucC, as well as describe a surface entropy reduction strategy that was employed for structure determination. Finally, we show solution X-ray scattering data that support a unique dimeric quaternary structure for IucC. These new insights into aerobactin assembly will help inform potential antivirulence strategies and advance our understanding of siderophore biosynthesis.</ce:simple-para>
               </ce:abstract-sec></ce:abstract>

            <ce:sections>
               <ce:section id="cesec10" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="cestitle10">Introduction</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="para10" view="all">
                     <ce:italic>Klebsiella pneumoniae</ce:italic> (KP)<ce:cross-ref id="cecref210" refid="FN2">
                        <ce:sup loc="post">3</ce:sup>
                     </ce:cross-ref>
					                <ce:footnote id="FN2">
                        <ce:label>3</ce:label>
                        <ce:note-para id="cenpara80" view="all">The abbreviations used are:</ce:note-para>
                        <ce:note-para id="cenpara90" view="all">
                           <ce:def-list id="deflist10">
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm10">KP</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes10">
                                 <ce:para id="para20" view="all">
                                    <ce:italic>Klebsiella pneumoniae</ce:italic>
                                 </ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm20">hvKP</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes20">
                                 <ce:para id="para30" view="all">hypervirulent pathotype KP</ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm30">NRPS</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes30">
                                 <ce:para id="para40" view="all">nonribosomal peptide synthetase</ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm40">NIS</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes40">
                                 <ce:para id="para50" view="all">NRPS-independent siderophore</ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm50">RMSD</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes50">
                                 <ce:para id="para60" view="all">root mean square displacement</ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm60">DL</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes60">
                                 <ce:para id="para70" view="all">disordered loop</ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm70">SER</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes70">
                                 <ce:para id="para80" view="all">surface entropy reduction</ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm80">hLys</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes80">
                                 <ce:para id="para90" view="all">
                                    <ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                                    <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-hydroxy-<ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysine</ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm90">ahLys</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes90">
                                 <ce:para id="para100" view="all">
                                    <ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                                    <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-acetyl-<ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                                    <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-hydroxy-<ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysine</ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                              <ce:def-term id="defterm100">SAXS</ce:def-term>
                              <ce:def-description id="cedefdes100">
                                 <ce:para id="para110" view="all">small-angle X-ray scattering.</ce:para>
                              </ce:def-description>
                           </ce:def-list>
                        </ce:note-para>
                     </ce:footnote> is a Gram-negative bacillus ubiquitously found in surface water and soil (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref220" refid="bib1">1</ce:cross-ref>). Besides this saprophytic existence, it is a common colonizer of the mucosal surfaces of the human gastrointestinal tract. However, KP has shown a propensity to transition from colonizer to opportunistic pathogen in susceptible individuals with underlying medical problems and compromised immune systems (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref230" refid="bib2">2</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref240" refid="bib3">3</ce:cross-ref>). Historically described as a cause of community-acquired pneumonia, KP is now recognized as one of the most common causative agents of hospital-acquired infections globally, capable of infiltrating the lungs, urinary tract, surgical wounds, and bloodstream. This has been particularly problematic for the medical community as nosocomial KP isolates are often highly drug-resistant, making these healthcare-associated infections especially difficult to treat (<ce:cross-refs id="cecrefs10" refid="bib4 bib5">4–</ce:cross-refs>
                     <ce:cross-ref id="cecref250" refid="bib6">6</ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para120" view="all">In addition to acquiring drug resistance, KP can also present with a novel hypervirulent pathotype (hvKP), which is now widely recognized by the medical community (<ce:cross-refs id="cecrefs20" refid="bib7 bib8">7–</ce:cross-refs>
                     <ce:cross-ref id="cecref260" refid="bib9">9</ce:cross-ref>). In 1986, a seminal report was published linking KP to severe life-threatening infections in ambulatory individuals from the community (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref270" refid="bib10">10</ce:cross-ref>). In the decades that followed, numerous reports from around the globe of KP-associated infections, including hepatic abscesses, endopthalmitis, meningitis, osteomyelitis, and necrotizing fasciitis, in otherwise healthy individuals, illustrated the emergence of this new hypervirulent pathotype (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref280" refid="bib11">11</ce:cross-ref>
                     <ce:cross-refs id="cecrefs30" refid="bib12 bib13 bib14 bib15 bib16 bib17">–</ce:cross-refs>
                     <ce:cross-ref id="cecref290" refid="bib18">18</ce:cross-ref>). Amid recent reports suggesting the confluence of both drug-resistance and hypervirulence determinants in clinical isolates of KP, medical professionals fear a true “super bug” may be evolving (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref300" refid="bib19">19</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref310" refid="bib20">20</ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para130" view="all">Investigations to understand the enhanced virulence of hvKP strains highlighted the importance of siderophore systems and iron acquisition (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref320" refid="bib21">21</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref330" refid="bib22">22</ce:cross-ref>). Because iron is a vital mineral nutrient, and bioavailable iron is often below the micromolar concentration required to support growth, siderophores are often considered critical virulence factors for pathogenic bacteria (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref340" refid="bib23">23</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref350" refid="bib24">24</ce:cross-ref>). hvKP is highly associated with a large virulence-conferring plasmid containing genes for the biosynthesis of aerobactin and the glycosylated enterobactin analogue salmochelin (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref360" refid="bib25">25</ce:cross-ref>). Combined with chromosomally encoded enterobactin and yersiniabactin operons, most strains of hvKP have the ability to produce four distinct siderophores. However, <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>in vivo</ce:italic> experiments have both demonstrated that of the four siderophores, knocking out the production of <ce:italic>only</ce:italic> aerobactin significantly diminished the virulence of hvKP (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref370" refid="bib26">26</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref380" refid="bib27">27</ce:cross-ref>). Although the mechanistic basis underlying the relative importance of aerobactin is unknown, there is growing appreciation for the differing roles (<ce:italic>e.g.</ce:italic> colonization <ce:italic>versus</ce:italic> invasion) siderophores can perform, as well as numerous examples of individual siderophores contributing significantly to pathogenicity when redundant siderophores are available (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref390" refid="bib24">24</ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para140" view="all">Based on the critical role of aerobactin in the evolution of a particularly worrisome new pathogen, we were motivated to revisit this early-described siderophore and study its biosynthesis in detail. Aerobactin, a mixed carboxylate-hydroxamate siderophore, was first isolated from the iron-deficient culture media of <ce:italic>Aerobacter aerogenes</ce:italic> by Gibson and Magrath in 1969 (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref400" refid="bib28">28</ce:cross-ref>). Genetic experiments in the early 1980s using the <ce:italic>Escherichia coli</ce:italic> ColV-K30 plasmid identified the aerobactin operon containing genes for four enzymes (“iron uptake chelate” <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic>) and an outer membrane receptor (“iron uptake transporter” <ce:italic>iutA</ce:italic>) (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref410" refid="bib29">29</ce:cross-ref>). By selectively knocking out and re-complementing <ce:italic>iuc</ce:italic> gene function, and detecting the resultant biosynthetic intermediates in culture supernatants, Neilands and co-workers (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref420" refid="bib30">30</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref430" refid="bib31">31</ce:cross-ref>) were able to decipher the aerobactin biosynthetic pathway, an effort they described as “ … the first entire gene-product-function analysis of any siderophore gene complex from any microbial species” (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref440" refid="fig1">Fig. 1</ce:cross-ref>
                     <ce:float-anchor refid="fig1"/>). Subsequent biochemical studies on isolated <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> IucD investigated and confirmed its proposed <ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysine <ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                     <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-monooxygenase activity (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref450" refid="bib32">32</ce:cross-ref>). Similarly, early studies on isolated <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> IucB were able to confirm its <ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                     <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-hydroxy-<ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysine (hLys) acetyltransferase activity (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref460" refid="bib33">33</ce:cross-ref>). However, functional characterization of the <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> aerobactin NIS synthetases IucA or IucC, the enzymes proposed to ligate 2 eq of the <ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                     <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-acetyl-<ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                     <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-hydroxy-<ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysine (ahLys) intermediate with citrate, was never reported. IucA and IucC belong to a family of ligases that have been defined as the nonribosomal peptide synthetase (NRPS)-independent siderophore (NIS) synthetases (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref470" refid="bib34">34</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref480" refid="bib35">35</ce:cross-ref>). IucA and IucC, respectively, represent the archetypal Type A (utilizing citrate as a substrate) and Type C (utilizing a citrate derivative) NIS synthetases. Furthermore, no one has described the <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic> reconstitution of aerobactin biosynthesis to functionally demonstrate the proposed pathway. Recent structural and functional studies of NIS synthetases, including hvKP IucA (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref490" refid="bib36">36</ce:cross-ref>), achromobactin synthetase AcsD (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref500" refid="bib37">37</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref510" refid="bib38">38</ce:cross-ref>), as well as petrobactin synthetases AsbA (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref520" refid="bib39">39</ce:cross-ref>) and AsbB (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref530" refid="bib40">40</ce:cross-ref>), have all advanced our understanding of the catalytic function of these closely-related enzymes.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para150" view="all">Herein, we present structural and functional investigations of aerobactin biosynthesis from a hvKP clinical isolate. First, we produced aerobactin through heterologous expression of the four <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic> genes in an aerobactin-deficient strain of <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic>. Second, using the four isolated and purified IucA–D enzymes, we were able to functionally reconstitute aerobactin biosynthesis <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic>. Additionally, we quantified pathway turnover frequency and demonstrated the high degree of stereoselectivity employed by the synthetases IucA and IucC. Finally, we report the steady-state kinetics, and structural characterization of IucC by X-ray crystallography and small-angle solution scattering. Together with our previous studies of IucA (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref540" refid="bib36">36</ce:cross-ref>), hvKP IucA and IucC represent the first pair of Type A and Type C NIS synthetases from a single pathway to be structurally and functionally characterized. This work furthers our understanding of NIS biosynthesis and provides the biochemical and structural foundation to further explore this potential antivirulence target.</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="cesec20" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="cestitle20">Results</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:section id="cesec30" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle30">Heterologous in vivo aerobactin production</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para160" view="all">Much of the prior investigation on aerobactin biosynthesis originated from a pathogenic strain of <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> harboring the aerobactin operon in the ColV-K30 plasmid (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref550" refid="bib29">29</ce:cross-ref>). Although Neilands and co-workers (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref560" refid="bib31">31</ce:cross-ref>) studied aerobactin biosynthesis by knocking out and re-complementing aerobactin genes originating from this plasmid, we employed a parallel approach in which an aerobactin-deficient <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> strain was engineered to produce the siderophore through heterologous co-expression of the four <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic> genes. When protein expression was induced from a plasmid containing <ce:italic>iucD</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>iucB</ce:italic>, ahLys was prominently detected in the culture supernatant by HPLC-MS (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref570" refid="fig2">Fig. 2</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:float-anchor refid="fig2"/>
                        <ce:italic>A</ce:italic>). However, as predicted, when expressing only the synthetase genes <ce:italic>iucA</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>iucC</ce:italic>, none of the aerobactin intermediates were detected (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref580" refid="fig2">Fig. 2</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>). Finally, when the <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> were transformed and induced to express all four <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic> genes, a pronounced peak corresponding to aerobactin was observed alongside secondary peaks corresponding to the intermediates ahLys and citryl-ahLys (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref590" refid="fig2">Fig. 2</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>C</ce:italic>). In addition to demonstrating aerobactin biosynthesis, this engineered system served as a relatively convenient/accessible source for isolating <ce:italic>in vivo</ce:italic> biosynthesized aerobactin and citryl-ahLys without using a pathogenic bacterial strain or requiring iron-deficient culture media. Although these metabolites were detected and isolated from the culture media supernatant, it is unclear whether they were actively secreted or if they accumulated via cell lysis or passive diffusion.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec40" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle40">In vitro aerobactin reconstitution</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para170" view="all">After demonstrating expression of the four <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic> genes was sufficient for aerobactin production <ce:italic>in vivo</ce:italic>, the pathway was investigated <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic> using purified enzymes (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref600" refid="fig3">Fig. 3</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:float-anchor refid="fig3"/>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref610" refid="ecomp10">Table S1 and Fig. S1</ce:cross-ref>). Despite a tendency to aggregate during expression, IucD, a putative membrane-associated enzyme, was purified as a monodisperse sample with the help of detergent. IucB, expressed as a cleavable chaperone chimera, had a broader size distribution and also had a tendency to aggregate at elevated concentrations and temperatures. However, when incubated together with substrates <ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysine, NADPH, acetyl-CoA, ATP, citrate, and the co-factor FAD, they were able to biosynthesize ahLys (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref620" refid="fig3">Fig. 3</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>). When IucD, IucB, and IucA were combined, all of the ahLys was ligated to citrate, forming the citryl-ahLys intermediate, but no aerobactin was detected (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref630" refid="fig3">Fig. 3</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>C</ce:italic>). In contrast, when IucD, IucB, and IucC were incubated together, no appreciable citryl-ahLys or aerobactin was detected (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref640" refid="fig3">Fig. 3</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>D</ce:italic>). Finally, when substrates and all four purified IucA–D enzymes were included in the reaction mixture, a prominent peak corresponding to aerobactin was observed alongside lesser amounts of ahLys and citryl-ahLys remaining in the mixture (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref650" refid="fig3">Fig. 3</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>E</ce:italic>). This <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic> reconstitution of aerobactin biosynthesis using purified enzymes provides direct functional evidence supporting the reported aerobactin pathway (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref660" refid="bib30">30</ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec50" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle50">Aerobactin pathway turnover frequency</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para180" view="all">In addition to establishing the competency of the <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic> aerobactin biosynthetic system, the turnover of the pathway was also examined quantitatively. Aerobactin was purified from the culture supernatant of the engineered <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> stain (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref670" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S2, <ce:italic>D–F</ce:italic>
                        </ce:cross-ref>) and used to construct a standard curve (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref680" refid="fig4">Fig. 4</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:float-anchor refid="fig4"/>
                        <ce:italic>A</ce:italic>). The reconstitution reaction was sampled along its time course to monitor how aerobactin evolved over time (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref690" refid="fig4">Fig. 4</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>). The linear time points on the reaction progress curves were fit with regression lines and used to estimate the pathway velocity (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref700" refid="fig4">Fig. 4</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>C</ce:italic>). By employing a relatively high substrate concentration (5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>) in the reconstitution, it was presumed that the resulting pathway kinetics would approximate saturating steady-state conditions. The reconstitution velocity was assessed at three different enzyme concentrations (2, 4, and 8 μ<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> of each individual enzyme). When doubling the enzyme concentrations, the pathway velocity only increased by ≈1.4. It is unclear why reaction velocity was not directly proportional to enzyme concentration; however, it could be related to the limited stability of IucB and IucD. An aerobactin pathway turnover frequency of 6 ± 2 min<ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup> was estimated by averaging the turnover numbers at the three different enzyme concentrations.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec60" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle60">Aerobactin synthetase stereoselectivity</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para190" view="all">The stereoselectivity of the aerobactin synthetases IucA and IucC has not been determined. Two distinct stereocenters are relevant in aerobactin biosynthesis. <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> IucD was previously shown to only be capable of hydroxylating <ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysine, and had no activity with <ce:small-caps>d</ce:small-caps>-lysine (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref710" refid="bib32">32</ce:cross-ref>). Therefore, it follows that the stereocenters derived from the α-carbon of lysine have the 2′<ce:italic>S</ce:italic> configuration in aerobactin and its precursors. Condensation of ahLys with one of the two <ce:italic>pro</ce:italic>-chiral carboxymethyl groups of citrate breaks the symmetry of the molecule and results in a second stereocenter at the citrate-derived C3 position in citryl-ahLys (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref720" refid="fig1">Fig. 1</ce:cross-ref>). Citryl-ahLys purified from <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic> co-expression eluted as a single peak by HPLC (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref730" refid="fig5">Fig. 5</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:float-anchor refid="fig5"/>
                        <ce:italic>A</ce:italic>). Similarly, citryl-ahLys isolated from the <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic> IucA-catalyzed condensation of ahLys and citrate also eluted as a single peak (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref740" refid="fig5">Fig. 5</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>). In contrast, a synthetic epimeric mixture of citryl-ahLys at the C3 position eluted as two distinct peaks, presumed to represent the two diastereomers (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref750" refid="fig5">Fig. 5</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>C</ce:italic>). Elution of authentic 3<ce:italic>R,</ce:italic>2′<ce:italic>S-</ce:italic> and 3<ce:italic>S,</ce:italic>2′<ce:italic>S</ce:italic>-enriched citryl-ahLys produced through total synthesis, featuring enantioenrichment of a citrate intermediate by fractional recrystallization, supported this presumption and allowed the absolute configuration of the two peaks to be assigned (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref760" refid="fig5">Fig. 5</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:italic>D</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>E</ce:italic>). The correlation of the retention times of the biosynthesized samples and the synthetic 3<ce:italic>S,</ce:italic>2′<ce:italic>S</ce:italic>-enriched sample indicated that only the 3<ce:italic>S,</ce:italic>2′<ce:italic>S-</ce:italic>diastereomer was appreciably produced by IucA. When biosynthesized citryl-ahLys was the substrate of IucC, all of it was turned over to make aerobactin (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref770" refid="fig5">Fig. 5</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:italic>A</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>). Conversely, when the synthetic citryl-ahLys samples were incubated with IucC, only the peak corresponding to the 3<ce:italic>S,</ce:italic>2′<ce:italic>S-</ce:italic>diastereomer diminished, whereas the peak corresponding to the 3<ce:italic>R,</ce:italic>2′<ce:italic>S-</ce:italic>diastereomer remained unperturbed (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref780" refid="fig5">Fig. 5</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:italic>C–E</ce:italic>). Taken together, these results demonstrate that the aerobactin synthetases are highly stereoselective; only 3<ce:italic>S,</ce:italic>2′<ce:italic>S</ce:italic>-citryl-ahLys is produced by IucA, and only 3<ce:italic>S,</ce:italic>2′<ce:italic>S</ce:italic>-citryl-ahLys is consumed by IucC in the production of aerobactin (<ce:cross-refs id="cecrefs40" refid="fig1 fig5">Figs. 1 and 5</ce:cross-refs>).</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec70" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle70">Steady-state kinetics of IucC</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para200" view="all">By using IucA to biosynthesize citryl-ahLys from synthetic ahLys, all three substrates were available to evaluate the steady-state kinetics of IucC (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref790" refid="tbl1">Table 1</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:float-anchor refid="tbl1"/>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref800" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S3</ce:cross-ref>). To conserve limited quantities of ahLys, hydroxylamine was employed as a saturating surrogate nucleophile when evaluating the activity of ATP and citryl-ahLys. IucC displayed relatively high-affinity for ATP (<ce:italic>K<ce:inf loc="post">m</ce:inf>
                        </ce:italic> = 170 ± 20 μ<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>) compared with its other two substrates citryl-ahLys (<ce:italic>K<ce:inf loc="post">m</ce:inf>
                        </ce:italic> = 1.32 ± 0.08 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>) and ahLys (<ce:italic>K<ce:inf loc="post">m</ce:inf>
                        </ce:italic> = 1.9 ± 0.2 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>). IucC showed similar turnover frequencies (<ce:italic>k</ce:italic>
                        <ce:inf loc="post">cat</ce:inf> = 25–35 min<ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup>) for all three substrates and, unsurprisingly, exhibited the highest velocity when consuming only native substrates. Overall, IucC displayed a relatively modest catalytic efficiency (<ce:italic>k<ce:inf loc="post">cat</ce:inf>/K<ce:inf loc="post">m</ce:inf>
                        </ce:italic> = 300–2,500 <ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>
                        <ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup> s<ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup>) in the production of aerobactin.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec80" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle80">X-ray crystal structure of IucC</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para210" view="all">Crystals of WT IucC were grown, but despite extensive optimization efforts, they were of insufficient quality to solve its X-ray crystal structure. To overcome this hurdle and grow better diffracting crystals, a surface entropy reduction strategy was employed (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref810" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S4</ce:cross-ref>). Using one of 12 engineered IucC surface mutants (designated M1–M12), the X-ray crystal structure of IucC surface mutant M5 (containing five substitutions, S182G, E183S, D185T, Q187G, and Q188T) was solved to 2.45-Å resolution by molecular replacement (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref820" refid="tbl2">Table 2</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:float-anchor refid="tbl2"/>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref830" refid="fig6">Fig. 6</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:float-anchor refid="fig6"/>).</ce:para>
                     <ce:para id="para220" view="all">The IucC monomeric tertiary structure is described using a cupped hand analogy (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref840" refid="fig6">Fig. 6</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>A</ce:italic>), following the convention employed for other structurally-characterized NIS synthetases (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref850" refid="bib38">38</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref860" refid="bib40">40</ce:cross-ref>). The thumb domain (residues 2–113) consists of a 3-helix bundle adjoined by a distorted 4-stranded antiparallel β-sheet. The extended loop region (114–163) contains a loop that is directed into the interior of the protein followed by four short α-helices. The fingers domain (164–362) comprises a 6-stranded and a 3-stranded anti-parallel β-sheet decorated with loops and α-helices. This domain also includes the mutated surface patch (182–188) that helped mediate crystallization. Finally, the palm domain (363–576) contains a prominent 3-helix bundle and forms the base of the central active site depression. The tertiary fold is homologous to the other structurally characterized NIS synthetases, including Type C AlcC (RMSD = 1.5 Å, 392 Cα), and AsbB (RMSD = 2.3 Å, 386 Cα), as well as Type A IucA (r.m.s. displacement = 4.1 Å, 361 Cα), and AcsD (RMSD = 5.1 Å, 315 Cα).</ce:para>
                     <ce:para id="para230" view="all">The IucC model has three gaps where disordered loops (DL1–3) were unresolved in the electron density map (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref870" refid="fig6">Fig. 6</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>A</ce:italic>). In <ce:cross-ref id="cecref880" refid="fig6">Fig. 6</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>, the three disordered loops were modeled onto the crystal structure to better appreciate their proximity to the putative active site. The first gap (DL1, ≈279–288) in the fingers domain encompasses a lid loop extending over the putative active site and has been shown to be important in binding ATP and citrate in the other structurally-characterized NIS synthetases. The second gap (DL2, ≈331–341) in the fingers domain is a surface loop connecting a β-sheet and a short α-helix. Finally, the third disordered loop (DL3, ≈550–562) occurred toward the C terminus in the palm domain. This loop is in close proximity to the putative active site and appears to have inherent flexibility, with gaps occurring in the corresponding regions of the homologous AcsD, AlcC, and IucA crystal structures. The IucC surface mutant M12 has an engineered patch (551–558) within this disordered loop region. The negligible catalytic activity of this mutant enzyme provides evidence for a potential functional role of this flexible region (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref890" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S5</ce:cross-ref>). ATP and Mg<ce:sup loc="post">2+</ce:sup> were also modeled into the putative active site to highlight the conserved binding mode that IucC and the other NIS synthetases appear to employ for this common substrate (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref900" refid="fig6">Fig. 6</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>). In this binding pose, several conserved residues including His-136, Ser-257, Arg-259, Thr-260, Lys-273, Thr-281, Arg-285, Arg-352, and His-422 are available in IucC to make specific interactions with the adenine, ribose, and phosphate portions of the nucleotide. Conserved residues Gln-424, Asn-425, and Asp-442 are also available to coordinate the Mg<ce:sup loc="post">2+</ce:sup> ion intimately contacting the phosphate groups of ATP (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref910" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S6</ce:cross-ref>). The unliganded active site and proximally-located disordered loops limit our ability to infer how ahLys and citryl-ahLys bind in the active site, as well as how IucC catalyzes their condensation.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec90" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle90">The quaternary structure of IucC</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para240" view="all">Features from the crystal lattice were combined with biochemical and solution-phase small-angle X-ray scattering (SAXS) data to investigate the oligomeric state and quaternary structure of IucC. Despite migrating a bit lower than expected by SDS-PAGE (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref920" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S1<ce:italic>A</ce:italic>
                        </ce:cross-ref>), MS indicated that the full construct was expressed, and had a monomeric mass of 66.1 kDa (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref930" refid="ecomp10">Table S1</ce:cross-ref>). During purification, IucC eluted from size exclusion chromatography primarily as a tetramer, partially overlapping a secondary dimer peak (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref940" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S1<ce:italic>D</ce:italic>
                        </ce:cross-ref>). After freezing, storing, and thawing tetrameric fractions, the tetrameric oligomer was observed to be stable (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref950" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S1, <ce:italic>B</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>C</ce:italic>
                        </ce:cross-ref>) and appeared the most crystallizable oligomer in screening experiments. Molecular weight calculations from SAXS data of IucC in standard storage buffer (≈230–240 kDa) slightly underestimated its theoretical tetrameric mass (264 kDa) (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref960" refid="ecomp10">Table S2</ce:cross-ref>). However, when SAXS data were collected on IucC in the presence of ATP, the calculated molecular mass was reduced significantly to ≈155 kDa, a value marginally overestimating a dimer (132 kDa). Size exclusion chromatography also illustrated the propensity of IucC to transition to a dimer upon exposure to its nucleotide substrate (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref970" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S1<ce:italic>E</ce:italic>
                        </ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
                     <ce:para id="para250" view="all">Inspection of the asymmetric unit containing eight IucC molecules revealed both plausible tetrameric and dimeric assemblies <ce:italic>in crystallo</ce:italic> (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref980" refid="fig6">Fig. 6</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>C</ce:italic>). The crystalline asymmetric unit can be reduced into two abutting tetramers adopting a circular configuration. This tetramer can be further divided into two identical dimers bearing resemblance to the dimeric assembly reported for the Type C NIS synthetases AlcC and AsbB (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref990" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S7</ce:cross-ref>) (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1000" refid="bib40">40</ce:cross-ref>). Comparison of the experimental IucC scattering curve to scattering curves calculated from potential crystalline assemblies revealed the strongest correlation with the tetramer (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1010" refid="fig7">Fig. 7</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:float-anchor refid="fig7"/>
                        <ce:italic>A</ce:italic>). Likewise, the size and shape of an <ce:italic>ab initio</ce:italic> molecular envelope calculated from the SAXS data were consistent with the general size and shape of the <ce:italic>in crystallo</ce:italic> tetramer (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1020" refid="fig7">Fig. 7</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>A</ce:italic>, <ce:italic>inset</ce:italic>). SAXS data collected on IucC in the presence of 1 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> ATP and MgCl<ce:inf loc="post">2</ce:inf> showed a marked reduction in the near zero-angle scatting intensity, indicating a reduction in particle mass (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1030" refid="fig7">Fig. 7</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>). In the conditions containing ATP, the SAXS data were most closely approximated by the curve calculated from the dimer, particularly in the low <ce:italic>q</ce:italic> region. Again, there is reasonable correlation between the crystalline dimer and an <ce:italic>ab inito</ce:italic> molecular envelope calculated from IucC in the presence of ATP (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1040" refid="fig7">Fig. 7</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>, <ce:italic>inset</ce:italic>). Nevertheless, inspection of the molecular envelopes in <ce:cross-ref id="cecref1050" refid="fig7">Fig. 7</ce:cross-ref> reveals that they are a bit too “snug” for the tetramer and a bit too “loose” for the dimer. Moreover, the molecular weight estimations seemed to underestimate a tetramer and overestimate a dimer (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1060" refid="ecomp10">Table S2</ce:cross-ref>). Both of these observations suggest IucC likely exists as a mixture of both tetramer and dimer under both conditions. Modeling the scattering curves as a mixture of the two oligomers revealed that the experimental data were best fit by a 91:9% mixture of tetramer:dimer in storage buffer, whereas it was best approximated as a 28:72% tetramer:dimer ratio in the presence of its substrate ATP.</ce:para>
                     <ce:para id="para260" view="all">Computational analysis of the crystal lattice (PISA (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1070" refid="bib41">41</ce:cross-ref>) and EPPIC (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1080" refid="bib42">42</ce:cross-ref>)) predicted the dimer was the only biologically relevant oligomeric assembly, burying ≈1,080 Å<ce:sup loc="post">2</ce:sup> of surface area between the two protomers. This prediction is also supported by the observation that all four dimers within the asymmetric unit are identical, whereas the structure of the two tetramers are marginally different (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1090" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S7, <ce:italic>A</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>B</ce:italic>
                        </ce:cross-ref>). Taken together, it appears IucC is capable of forming both a tetramer and dimer in solution. However, although the tetrameric assembly appears to promote crystallization, it is more likely an artifactual consequence of heterologous expression and purification, with the biologically relevant dimer predominating in the presence of its substrate ATP.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="cesec100" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="cestitle100">Discussion</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="para270" view="all">Heterologous expression of the four <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic> genes in an aerobactin-deficient strain of <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> enabled the production of the siderophore. This implies that all the necessary substrates can be harvested from the cellular metabolic pool, not requiring any additional enzymatic activity to supply the pathway. It is interesting to note that the operon of the closely related, and virulence-promoting, NIS staphyloferrin B contains specialized enzymes dedicated to citrate and amino acid precursor production (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1100" refid="bib43">43</ce:cross-ref>). The relatively low burden of the 5-gene, 8-kb aerobactin operon likely contributes to its relatively wide distribution in pathogenic members of the Enterobacteriaceae family, including <ce:italic>Escherichia</ce:italic>, <ce:italic>Klebsiella</ce:italic>, <ce:italic>Salmonella</ce:italic>, <ce:italic>Yersinia</ce:italic>, <ce:italic>Enterobacter</ce:italic>, <ce:italic>Shigella</ce:italic>, <ce:italic>Serratia</ce:italic>, and <ce:italic>Citrobacter</ce:italic>, as well as marine <ce:italic>Vibrio</ce:italic> (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1110" refid="bib44">44</ce:cross-ref>) species.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para280" view="all">The four purified IucA–D enzymes were also able to reconstitute aerobactin biosynthesis <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic>. Although hLys was not detected in the assay when only IucD was included, the three downstream metabolites ahLys, citryl-ahLys, and aerobactin were all clearly identified when additional Iuc enzymes were added. Enzyme combinations in which either IucD or IucB were omitted did not lead to the identification of nonhydroxylated or nonacetylated aerobactin analogues, respectively (data not shown). This observation demonstrates the aerobactin secondary metabolic pathway is engrained with a high-degree of substrate selectivity to limit production of nonfunctional analogues. Both of the synthetases are required to synthesize aerobactin; neither was capable of compensating for the activity of the other to any appreciable extent. This is in contrast to the Type C NIS synthetase AsbB in petrobactin biosynthesis, which was reported to partially compensate for the absence of its Type A counterpart AsbA (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1120" refid="bib40">40</ce:cross-ref>). Although having one synthetase capable of catalyzing both condensations would be ideal from a metabolic efficiency perspective, the presence of two distinct synthetases in aerobactin biosynthesis, as well as many of the other NIS pathways, likely implies a difficult evolutionary challenge of designing a single stereoselective active site to accommodate both sets of substrates. One unique way of addressing this challenge would be by combining two synthetase active sites into a single polypeptide, an approach that may be adopted by the putative NIS synthetase CiuE in the biosynthesis of corynebactin, a lysine and citrate-based siderophore closely-related to aerobactin (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1130" refid="bib45">45</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref1140" refid="bib46">46</ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para290" view="all">Functional characterization of the Type A NIS synthetase AcsD described a highly stereoselective mechanism for the condensation of citrate and <ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-serine (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1150" refid="bib38">38</ce:cross-ref>). Furthermore, structural models of the AcsD and IucA active sites predicted a mechanism in which only one of the two <ce:italic>pro</ce:italic>-chiral carboxymethyl groups of citrate is activated by ATP to form a citryl-adenylate intermediate (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1160" refid="bib36">36</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref1170" refid="bib38">38</ce:cross-ref>). Experiments presented herein confirmed the structural predictions by functionally demonstrating the stereoselective mechanisms of IucA and IucC. Although the final symmetry of aerobactin renders the citrate-derived C3 position achiral, stereochemistry at the C3 position has been shown to play a critical role in influencing siderophore potency and iron affinity in the NIS staphyloferrin B (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1180" refid="bib43">43</ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para300" view="all">In addition to demonstrating aerobactin biosynthesis <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic>, the velocity of the pathway was determined to more quantitatively evaluate aerobactin production and compare it to other reconstituted pathways. The aerobactin turnover frequency (6 ± 2 min<ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup>) is similar to values reported for the <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic> production of the siderophores pyochelin (2 min<ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup>) (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1190" refid="bib47">47</ce:cross-ref>) and yersiniabactin (1.4 min<ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup>) (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1200" refid="bib48">48</ce:cross-ref>), despite employing contrasting NRPS and hybrid NRPS/polyketide synthetase enzymatic mechanisms, respectively. The apparent steady-state kinetics of the individual aerobactin synthetase IucC was also examined quantitatively. IucC showed an affinity for ATP similar to IucA (<ce:italic>K<ce:inf loc="post">m</ce:inf>
                     </ce:italic> = 130 ± 30 μ<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>) (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1210" refid="bib36">36</ce:cross-ref>), a result supporting the common ATP binding mechanism that NIS synthetases are predicted to share. The relatively low catalytic efficiency of IucC also appeared comparable with values reported for the Type C NIS synthetase homologue AsbB (240 <ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>
                     <ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup> s<ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup>) (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1220" refid="bib40">40</ce:cross-ref>). These seemingly modest pathway turnover frequencies and enzyme catalytic efficiencies support the premise that high production may not be essential for these siderophore pathways to meet the demand for iron.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para310" view="all">Although WT hvKP IucC was a stable enzyme and expressed robustly, a surface entropy reduction mutant (M5) was required to grow high-quality crystals. Computational analysis of the resulting crystal lattice using EPPIC (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1230" refid="bib42">42</ce:cross-ref>) revealed that residues within the mutated surface patch (182–188) contributed to one-third (5 of 15) of the distinct crystal lattice interfaces, while not disturbing the predicted biologically relevant dimer interface or catalytic competency (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1240" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S5</ce:cross-ref>). This significant contribution to crystal lattice contacts helps rationalize how this particular mutant patch was able to promote higher quality crystal growth. Additionally, this most successful surface mutant had the highest melting temperature off all the evaluated IucC constructs, consistent with the commonly-held assertion that maximizing protein stability promotes successful crystallization (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1250" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S4<ce:italic>C</ce:italic>
                     </ce:cross-ref>) (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1260" refid="bib49">49</ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para320" view="all">Attempts to obtain a co-crystal structure with substrates, especially with ATP, were surprisingly unsuccessful, given the co-crystallization success of the other NIS synthetases. Although the underlying reasons behind this phenomenon are uncertain, it was observed that the disordered loops (DL1–3) in proximity to the active site all faced toward the interior of the asymmetric unit. Given the presumed active site flexibility required to bind the relatively complex substrates ahLys and citryl-ahLys, it is possible that inter-particle interactions from higher-order oligomerization, especially <ce:italic>in crystallo</ce:italic>, could have disrupted stable substrate binding. The dimeric quaternary structure predicted to be biologically relevant for IucC is similar, but clearly distinct from the assembly shared by its Type C homologues AlcC and AsbB (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1270" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S7<ce:italic>C</ce:italic>
                     </ce:cross-ref>). This represents the fourth distinct quaternary structure among the five structurally characterized NIS synthetases. Considering their high monomeric structural homology, their varied quaternary structure is noteworthy, despite its biological relevance remaining uncertain.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="para330" view="all">Motivated by the role aerobactin is reported to play in the newly recognized hvKP, the biosynthesis of the siderophore was revisited. Building upon pioneering studies in the 1980s, the aerobactin biosynthetic pathway was functionally demonstrated while attempting to further our understanding of a key virulence factor and of NIS biosynthesis in general. Even with the results of the current study, a number of inquiries related to aerobactin biosynthesis remain open: the detailed active site architecture and substrate binding geometry of IucC, what, if any, cellular localization or multienzyme assembly exists for the pathway, why it appears to be so important for mediating virulence in hvKP, and if it can be inhibited in an anti-virulence therapeutic approach.</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="cesec110" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="cestitle110">Experimental procedures</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:section id="cesec120" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle120">Cloning, expression, and purification of hvKP Iuc enzymes</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para340" view="all">Details of Iuc enzyme expression and purification are outlined in <ce:cross-ref id="cecref1280" refid="ecomp10">Table S1</ce:cross-ref>; a general expression and purification procedure is described below. The four <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic> genes were individually amplified from the hvKP1 clinical isolate (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1290" refid="bib50">50</ce:cross-ref>) genomic DNA using primers to incorporate restriction sites at the 5′ and 3′ ends of the gene. The genes were subcloned into modified expression vectors with N-terminal His tags and protease recognition sites for tag removal. The vectors were transformed into <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> BL21(DE3) for protein production. Cells were grown in LB media (250 rpm) to an <ce:italic>A</ce:italic>
                        <ce:inf loc="post">600 nm</ce:inf> of ≈0.5–0.7. Expression was induced with the addition of isopropyl 1-thio-β-<ce:small-caps>d</ce:small-caps>-galactopyranoside, followed by incubation for ≈18 h overnight at 16 °C. Cells were harvested by centrifugation at 6,000 × <ce:italic>g</ce:italic> for 15 min at 4 °C. After decanting off the supernatant media, the cell pellets were flash frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at −80 °C for later use.</ce:para>
                     <ce:para id="para350" view="all">Frozen cell pellets were re-suspended in lysis buffer and lysed by sonication. The resulting slurry was separated by ultracentrifugation at 185,000 × <ce:italic>g</ce:italic>. The supernatant was filtered over a 0.45-μm polysulfone membrane before being subjected to immobilized metal affinity chromatography. The lysate supernatant was passed over a 5-ml Ni<ce:sup loc="post">2+</ce:sup>- nitrilotriacetic acid column. Bound proteins were eluted from the column using lysis buffer plus 300 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> imidazole. Fractions that contained His-tagged Iuc enzymes by SDS-PAGE were combined and dialyzed overnight at 4 °C or room temperature with the appropriate protease in dialysis buffer for His/chaperone tag removal. After spiking with imidazole to 20 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>, the dialyzed sample was passed over the Ni<ce:sup loc="post">2+</ce:sup>- nitrilotriacetic acid column for a second time. The flow-through fractions containing the desired protein without the His tag were combined and concentrated using an appropriate centrifugal filter before being subjected to size exclusion chromatography (HiLoad<ce:sup loc="post">TM</ce:sup> 16/60 Superdex<ce:sup loc="post">TM</ce:sup> 200, GE Healthcare Life Sciences). The desired fractions were combined, concentrated, and flash frozen in liquid nitrogen before being stored at −80 °C for subsequent use.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec130" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle130">Heterologous in vivo aerobactin production</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para360" view="all">The four <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic> genes were subcloned into duet expression vectors for simultaneous co-expression. The <ce:italic>iucB</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>iucD</ce:italic> genes were subcloned into MCS1 (NcoI and BamHI) and MCS2 (NdeI and XhoI) of the CDFDuet-1 expression plasmid (Strep<ce:sup loc="post">R</ce:sup>/Spec<ce:sup loc="post">R</ce:sup>), respectively. Similarly, the <ce:italic>iucA</ce:italic> and <ce:italic>iucC</ce:italic> genes were subcloned into MCS1 (NcoI and BamHI) and MCS2 (NdeI and BglII) of the pETDuet-1 expression plasmid (Amp<ce:sup loc="post">R</ce:sup>), respectively. The <ce:italic>iucA</ce:italic> gene construct included a N-terminal His tag, whereas the other three enzymes were untagged. <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> BL21(DE3) were then transformed with either CDFDuet-1, pETDuet-1, or both of the plasmids. The transformed <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> strains were grown in 60 ml of M9 minimal media with appropriate antibiotics to an <ce:italic>A</ce:italic>
                        <ce:inf loc="post">600 nm</ce:inf> of ≈0.6–0.8 (37 °C, 250 rpm). Protein expression was induced with the addition of 750 μ<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> isopropyl 1-thio-β-<ce:small-caps>d</ce:small-caps>-galactopyranoside, and cells were incubated for ≈18 h overnight. The cells were pelleted by centrifugation at 4,500 rpm for 10 min, and the culture supernatant was decanted and frozen at −20 °C for subsequent analysis.</ce:para>
                     <ce:para id="para370" view="all">The supernatant was diluted 1:5 in 5% acetonitrile in water before being analyzed by HPLC-MS. Ten microliters of sample was injected over a 3 × 50-mm Agilent InfinityLab Poroshell 120 EC-C18 2.7 μm column held at 30 °C with a flow rate of 0.4 ml/min. The mobile phases consisted of 0.1% formic acid in water (A) and 0.1% formic acid in acetonitrile (B). The elution gradient started with isocratic 5% B for 2 min, followed by a linear gradient to 20% B over 10 min. The eluant was monitored for UV absorbance at 218 nm and by an in-line Advion Expression<ce:sup loc="post">L</ce:sup> compact mass spectrometer configured for positive mode electrospray ionization. The mass spectrometer was further configured with the following parameters: scan range: 100–1,000 <ce:italic>m</ce:italic>/<ce:italic>z</ce:italic>; capillary voltage: 150 V; ESI voltage +3,500 V; capillary temperature: 150 °C; gas temperature: 350 °C; gas flow: 4 liter/min.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec140" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle140">In vitro aerobactin reconstitution</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para380" view="all">A 100-μl reaction mixture was prepared containing 50 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> HEPES pH 7.5, 5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> MgCl<ce:inf loc="post">2</ce:inf>, 50 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> NaCl, 0.2 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> tris(2-carboxyethyl)phosphine, 5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> 
                        <ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysine, 5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> NADPH, 0.5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> FAD, 5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> acetyl-CoA, 5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> ATP, 5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> citrate, and various combinations of the Iuc enzymes at 10 μ<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> concentration. After incubating at 37 °C, 10-μl aliquots of the reaction mixture were removed at specified time points (up to 1 h) and quenched by the addition of 40 μl of 0.1% formic acid and 5% acetonitrile in water. The sample was briefly centrifuged to remove any precipitated protein before being analyzed using the same HPLC-MS procedure outlined above.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec150" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle150">Purification of biosynthesized citryl-ahLys and aerobactin</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para390" view="all">The general purification strategy was informed by the work of Butler and colleagues (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1300" refid="bib44">44</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref1310" refid="bib51">51</ce:cross-ref>). After acidifying to pH 2.5 using concentrated HCl, 250 ml of M9 minimal media culture supernatant from <ce:italic>iucA–D</ce:italic> co-expression was stirred with 10 g of AmberLite XAD-4 resin overnight at 4 °C. The resin was filtered from the supernatant and rinsed with 30 ml of water before being eluted with 100% methanol. Fractions evidenced to contain iron chelators by the liquid chrome azurol S assay (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1320" refid="bib52">52</ce:cross-ref>) were combined and concentrated to a residue (≈120 mg) under vacuum. This crude residue was re-dissolved in 1 ml of 5% acetonitrile in water, centrifuged to remove any remaining solids, and 50–100 μl was eluted over an Agilent ZORBAX SB-C18 9.4 × 250 mm 5-μm semi-preparative HPLC column held at 30 °C with a flow rate of 4 ml/min. The same mobile phase composition and elution gradient was employed as described above. Fractions from multiple injections shown to contain the iron chelators citryl-ahLys and aerobactin by the chrome azurol S assay and analytical HPLC-MS analysis were combined and reduced to dryness by rotary evaporation. From ≈250 ml of culture supernatant, 3.5 mg of citryl-ahLys and 7.2 mg of aerobactin was isolated with high purity (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1330" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S2</ce:cross-ref>). A standard curve for the quantitation of aerobactin production was constructed by analyzing known quantities of this aerobactin standard using the same HPLC method described above to quantify peak area.</ce:para>
                     <ce:para id="para400" view="all">
                        <ce:italic>In vitro</ce:italic> biosynthesized citryl-ahLys was isolated by scaling-up the IucA-catalyzed reaction. A 2.5-ml reaction mixture was prepared containing 10 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> HEPES pH 7.5, 5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> MgCl<ce:inf loc="post">2</ce:inf>, 50 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> NaCl, 27.5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> ATP, 27.5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> citrate, 25 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> ahLys, and 10 μ<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> IucA, and incubated at 37 °C for ≈15 h overnight. The enzyme was removed by filtration through a centrifugal filter and the filtrate was reduced to dryness under vacuum. The crude residue was re-dissolved in 5% acetonitrile in water before being purified by the identical semi-preparative HPLC procedure outlined above. The combined yield of the reaction and purification was 65%.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec160" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle160">Preparation of synthetic ahLys and citryl-ahLys</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para410" view="all">The detailed synthesis of aerobactin precursors ahLys and citryl-ahLys is presented in the supporting information (<ce:inter-ref id="interrefs50" xlink:href="http://www.jbc.org/cgi/content/full/RA118.002798/DC1" xlink:type="simple">Data S10–S25</ce:inter-ref>). Briefly, the <ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                        <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-oxidized and acetylated <ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysine derivative, ahLys was synthesized in accordance with previously described methods reported by Hu and Miller (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1340" refid="bib53">53</ce:cross-ref>). Late stage divergence from the reported route allowed for functionalization of the α-amino moiety of the fully protected ahLys derivative (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1350" refid="fig8">Fig. 8</ce:cross-ref>
                        <ce:float-anchor refid="fig8"/>). Enantiopure (<ce:italic>S</ce:italic>)-1,3-dimethyl citrate was prepared by co-crystallization with (<ce:italic>R</ce:italic>)-methylbenzylamine (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1360" refid="bib54">54</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref1370" refid="bib55">55</ce:cross-ref>). Subsequent activation to the corresponding citryl-chloride and coupling to the α-amine of the protected ahLys derivative yielded methyl-<ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                        <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-acetoxy-<ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                        <ce:sup loc="post">6</ce:sup>-acetyl-<ce:italic>N</ce:italic>
                        <ce:sup loc="post">2</ce:sup>-[(<ce:italic>S</ce:italic>)-1,3-dimethylcitryl]-<ce:small-caps>l</ce:small-caps>-lysinate. Global saponification with lithium hydroxide afforded the penultimate aerobactin intermediate (3<ce:italic>S,</ce:italic>2′<ce:italic>S</ce:italic>)-citryl-ahLys with minor epimerization.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec170" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle170">IucC steady-state kinetics</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para420" view="all">A coupled NADH oxidation assay was employed to evaluate the steady-state kinetics of IucC (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1380" refid="bib56">56</ce:cross-ref>). Reaction mixtures (50 μl) were prepared containing 50 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> HEPES pH 7.5, 15 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> MgCl<ce:inf loc="post">2</ce:inf>, 3 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> PEP, 500 μ<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> NADH, 10 units/ml of pyruvate kinase, lactate dehydrogenase, and adenylate kinase, 2 μ<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> IucC, and varied concentrations of substrates. The concentration of a single substrate was varied while the other two were at saturating/near saturating concentrations. When not being varied, ATP and citryl-ahLys were at 5 and 6.5 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>, respectively. When hydroxylamine was employed as a surrogate nucleophile, it was at 75 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>. Reactions were carried out in triplicate in 96-well half-area clear polystyrene plates at 37 °C; the absorbance (340 nm, ϵ = 6,220 <ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps>
                        <ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup> cm<ce:sup loc="post">−1</ce:sup>) was measured every 10–15 s using a Biotek Synergy 4 plate reader for 10 min. The maximum initial absorbance slopes were converted to enzyme activity and plotted <ce:italic>versus</ce:italic> varying substrate concentrations. The plots were then fit with nonlinear Michaelis-Menten regression in GraphPad Prism to calculate <ce:italic>V</ce:italic>
                        <ce:inf loc="post">max</ce:inf> and <ce:italic>K<ce:inf loc="post">m</ce:inf>
                        </ce:italic> values.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec180" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle180">IucC surface entropy reduction (SER) and crystallization</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para430" view="all">The general SER approach was informed by numerous literature reports, two of which are cited here (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1390" refid="bib57">57</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref1400" refid="bib58">58</ce:cross-ref>). A homology model of hvKP IucC was created based on the crystal structures of the two Type C NIS synthetases AsbB and AlcC using YASARA software. The homology model was visually inspected to identify sequences on the surface of the enzyme enriched in high-entropy charged/polar residues, including Lys, Glu, Gln, Arg, Asp, and Asn. Mutations were then designed to introduce lower entropy side chains (Ala, Gly, Ser, Thr, Tyr, and His) to promote the formation of stable crystal lattice contacts, while trying to avoid making the surface of the enzyme too hydrophobic. In total, 12 constructs were designed with a single mutated surface patch (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1410" refid="ecomp10">Fig. S4<ce:italic>B</ce:italic>
                        </ce:cross-ref>). The hvKP <ce:italic>iucC</ce:italic> plasmid was sent to GENEWIZ, Inc. (South Plainfield, NJ) to synthesize and sequence the 12 mutant genes. <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> BL21(DE3) were transformed with the vectors containing the mutant IucC genes, and protein expression and purification was carried out using the same general procedure described for WT IucC.</ce:para>
                     <ce:para id="para440" view="all">Of the 12 surface mutant proteins designed, seven were successfully purified in sufficient quantity for crystallization screening using a 120-condition in-house screen and the 1,536-condition high-throughput crystallization screening service available at the Hauptman-Woodward Medical Research Institute (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1420" refid="bib59">59</ce:cross-ref>). The thermal stability of WT IucC and the seven successfully purified SER mutants was assayed using a fluorescence-based thermal shift assay (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1430" refid="bib60">60</ce:cross-ref>). Although crystallization leads were identified for all seven of the surface mutants, initial crystals from surface mutant M5 appeared most promising. Conditions were optimized to ≈5.5 mg/ml of IucC M5 combined 1:1 with a mixture of 10–11% PEG 20,000 and 100 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> MES, pH 6.5, in hanging-drop vapor-diffusion format at 20 °C. Microseeding was employed to control nucleation and promote the growth of single crystals. Crystals were cryo-protected by serial transfer through mixture plus 8, 16, and 24% ethylene glycol before being flash frozen in liquid nitrogen.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec190" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle190">X-ray data collection, structure determination, and refinement</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para450" view="all">Frozen crystals were shipped to GM/CA ID-B at the Advanced Photon Source (APS) at the Argonne National Laboratory (Lemont, IL) for remote X-ray diffraction data collection. A total of 400 images were collected on a single crystal using a 100 × 45 μm beam (no attenuation) over 360° (0.9°, 0.5 s) on a Dectris Eiger-16m detector at a distance of 350 mm. The diffraction images were processed using iMosflm (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1440" refid="bib61">61</ce:cross-ref>) and the structure was solved by molecular replacement using Phaser (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1450" refid="bib62">62</ce:cross-ref>) software and employing monomeric AlcC (PDB code <ce:inter-ref id="interrefs60" xlink:href="pdb:3X0Q" xlink:type="simple">3X0Q</ce:inter-ref>, 29.9% identity) as the search model. Automated model building was performed using PHENIX.Autobuild (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1460" refid="bib63">63</ce:cross-ref>), manual model building and refinement was carried out using Coot (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1470" refid="bib64">64</ce:cross-ref>), and automated structure refinement was executed using PHENIX.Refine (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1480" refid="bib65">65</ce:cross-ref>). Data collection and refinement statistics are presented in <ce:cross-ref id="cecref1490" refid="tbl2">Table 2</ce:cross-ref>. In addition to the disordered loops discussed in the text, the protein chain was modeled for residues 542–545 despite their weak electron density. Structure factors and coordinates for hvKP IucC surface mutant M5 have been deposited in the Protein Data Bank (code <ce:inter-ref id="interrefs70" xlink:href="pdb:6CN7" xlink:type="simple">6CN7</ce:inter-ref>).</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
                  <ce:section id="cesec200" view="all">
                     <ce:section-title id="cestitle200">SAXS</ce:section-title>
                     <ce:para id="para460" view="all">SAXS data on WT hvKP IucC were collected at Stanford Synchrotron Radiation Lightsource (SSRL) beamline 4–2 (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1500" refid="bib66">66</ce:cross-ref>, <ce:cross-ref id="cecref1510" refid="bib67">67</ce:cross-ref>). Protein samples (40 μl) were prepared in a dilution series of 5.3, 4.0, 2.7, and 1.3 mg/ml in a buffer of 50 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> HEPES, 150 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> NaCl, and 0.2 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> tris(2-carboxyethyl)phosphine. To investigate the effect of ATP on the conformation of IucC, a second dilution series (5.1, 3.8, 2.5, and 1.3 mg/ml) was prepared in an identical buffer system plus 1 m<ce:small-caps>m</ce:small-caps> ATP and MgCl<ce:inf loc="post">2</ce:inf>. Each sample was held at 10 °C and data were collected with 20 exposures of 1-s duration. Scattering from a buffer blank was subtracted from the data. The scattering curves were produced using SASTool software (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1520" refid="bib67">67</ce:cross-ref>) and the scattering curves were analyzed using software from the ATSAS program package (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1530" refid="bib68">68</ce:cross-ref>): PRIMUSQT, DAMMIF, DAMAVER, CRYSOL, SUPCOMB, OLIGOMER, and DATVC. Guinier analysis (0.4 ≤ <ce:italic>q</ce:italic>·<ce:italic>R<ce:inf loc="post">G</ce:inf>
                        </ce:italic> ≤ 1.3) was used to determine the zero-angle scattering intensity (<ce:italic>I</ce:italic>
                        <ce:inf loc="post">0</ce:inf>). This value was then used to calculate the molecular mass by employing lysozyme (14.3 kDa, 16.3 mg/ml) as a reference standard. For molecular weight calculations based on Porod volume, the pair distance distribution (<ce:italic>P(r</ce:italic>)) function (<ce:italic>q</ce:italic> ≤ 0.3) was employed to calculate the Porod volume, which was then divided by 1.66 to yield the molecular mass in Da (<ce:cross-ref id="cecref1540" refid="bib69">69</ce:cross-ref>). The DATVC software was used to calculate the molecular weight by the volume of correlation (<ce:italic>V<ce:inf loc="post">C</ce:inf>
                        </ce:italic>) including data for <ce:italic>q</ce:italic> ≤ 0.3. The IucC <ce:italic>ab initio</ce:italic> molecular envelopes were calculated using P222 (without ATP) and P2 (with ATP) symmetry restraints in DAMMIF and including data for <ce:italic>q</ce:italic> ≤ 0.3. The CRYSOL scattering curves were calculated using the solvent electron density of water (0.334 <ce:italic>e</ce:italic>
                        <ce:sup loc="post">−</ce:sup>/Å<ce:sup loc="post">3</ce:sup>). To correct for a slight concentration dependence (minor interparticle repulsion) in the SAXS data, PRIMUSQT was used to generate scattering curves extrapolated to infinite dilution.</ce:para>
                  </ce:section>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="cesec210" view="all">
